                  <h1>
          Full text of "<a href="/details/gov.acus.1958.report">1958 Annual Report of the Office of Administrative Procedure</a>"
        </h1>
        <h2 class="pull-right">
          <small><a href="/details/gov.acus.1958.report">See other formats</a></small>
        </h2>
        <br class="clearfix" clear="right"/>
      <pre>ANNUAL REPORT 

of the 

OFFICE OF ADMINISTRATIVE PROCEDURE 



1958 






OFFICE OF LEGAL COUNSEL 
DEPARTMENT OF JUSTICE 

Washington, D. C, 



FOREWORD 

This Second Annual Report of the Office of Ad- 
ministrative Procedure covers the year ending 
December 31, 1958o 

Part I contains a summary of the major activities 
of the Office during the year. In this area the most 
significant development has been the growing fre- 
quency of requests for informal advice and opinions 
in the course of agency revision or promulgation of 
rules and regulations, and in the preparation of 
legislative proposals having a bearing on administra- 
tive procedures. 

Part II is the statistical section of the report. The 
tables set forth certain fundamental information on 
backlog, volume » and time with respect to proceedings 
pending before 21 Federal departments and agencies 
during fiscal year 1958. 

The statistics in Tables 7 through 12, relating to 

I the hearing examiner function, are similar to those 

*- compiled in the 1957 Annual Report and have been set 

/&lt;&gt; forth in a manner permitting comparison. In addition, 

-1 the scope of the study was enlarged to include certain 

basic statistics relating to the period prior to the 

assignment of proceedings to hearing exanniners and 

to their processing by the agencies after disposition 

by hearing examiners (Tables 1 through 6). 

In speaking of judicial statistics. Chief Justice 
Stone said: *'The statistical method of dealing with 
social problems often cannot be relied upon as a 
mathematical demonstration leading to spoken con- 
clusions, but it may be used to indicate tendencies, 
to mark out the boundaries of a problem, and to point 
out the directions to be given to a particular investi- 
gation of a non- statistical character.*' It is in this 
context and with this purpose in nnind that these 
tables have been compiled. 

In carrying out its responsibilities and in the 

preparation of this report, the Office acknowledges 

the helpful assistance provided by the Bureau of the 

f Budget and expresses its appreciation to the depart- 

. I ments and agencies for their continuing support and 

W cooperation. 



STAFF OF THE 
OFFICE OF ADMINISTRATIVE PROCEDURE

OFFICE OF THE ATTORNEY GENERAL 

[Order 142-57] 

Establishment of Office of Administrative Procedure 

Establishment of Office of Administrative Procedure . 
1. There is hereby established in the Department of Justice 
an Office of Administrative Procedure which shall be a 
component of and under the adnninistrative supervision of the 
Office of Legal Counsel. 

Director . 2. The Office shall be headed by a Director 
who will act in an advisory capacity in carrying out the 
purposes of the Office. 

Purposes of the Office . 3. With a view to achieving im- 
provements in administrative procedures within the Executive 
departments and agencies of the Federal Government, the 
Office shall: 

(a) Carry on continuous studies of the adequacy of the 
procedures by which Federal departments and agencies 
determine the rights, duties, and privileges of persons; 

(b) Initiate cooperative effort aniong the departments and 
agencies and their respective bars to develop and adopt so 
far as practicable uniform rules of practice and procedure; 

(c) Collect and publish facts and statistics concerning the 
procedures of the departments and agencies; 

(d) Assist departments and agencies in the formulation 
and improvement of their administrative procedures. 

Cooperation by Departnrients and Agencies, the Bar, and 
other interested persons . 4. The Director, in consultation 
with the Attorney General, is authorized to nnake appropriate 
arrangennents for securing the cooperation and advice of 
representatives of the departments and agencies, the bar, 
and other interested persons in connection with the per- 
formance of his duties. 

Reports . 5. The Director shall render an annual report 
for circulation to the departments and agencies, and such 
other reports as the Attorney General may from time to 
tinne request. 

Dated: February 6, 1957, 



Herbert Brownell, Jr. 
Attorney General

IX 



ANNUAL REPORT 

of the 

OFFICE OF ADMINISTRATIVE PROCEDURE 

1958 



PART I 

ORGANIZATION AND STATISTICAL SUMMARY 

The Office of Administrative Procedure, headed 
by a Director, is a component of the Office of Legal 
Counsel in the Department of Justice. Through con- 
tinuing studies, the collection and dissemination of 
facts and statistics, and advisory services it is the 
purpose of the Office to seek improvement in the 
administrative procedures whereby the Executive 
departnnents and agencies determine, either by rule- 
making or adjudication, the rights, privileges and 
duties of members of the public. 

To assist in carrying out these responsibilities, 
each of 48 departments and agencies has cooperated 
with the Office by designating a responsible staff 
officer to maintain official liaison. This arrangement 
not only serves as a point of contact with the agen- 
cies, but provides the Office with current information 
relating to the major activities of the respective 
agencies in the field of administrative procedure. 
The Office is also in frequent contact with bar asso- 
ciations, specialized agency bar groups, and other 
organizations and individuals interested in adminis- 
trative practice and procedures. 

During 1958, 117 assignments were processed by 
the Office, falling into the following general cate- 
gories: 43 reviews of legislative proposals, Z3 special 
assignnnents, 21 interagency studies and advisory 
opinions, 14 rulemaking proceedings, and 16 inter- 
agency conferences. In addition, a number of requests 
for advice and assistance were handled on a day-to- 
day infornnal basis. The range of these assignments 
extends from study and advice with respect to possible 
change in one rule to the revision or preparation of 



rules for the conduct of agency hearings. They include 
assistance in the drafting of legislation and the 
providing of views on the form and legality of such 
proposals. 

RECOGNITION AND DISCIPLINE OF ATTORNEYS 

One of the early recommendations of the Office 
was a proposal that all agencies dispense with formal 
admission procedures for attorneys and adopt in lieu 
thereof a proposed uniform rule which would make 
eligible to practice any person who is a member in 
good standing of the bar of the Supreme Court of the 
United States or of the highest court of a state, 
territory, insular possession, or District of Columbia 
and is not under a court order suspending, enjoining, 
restraining, disbarring, or otherwise restricting him 
in practicing law. At the time the recommendation 
was made 10 agencies had rules for formal admission 
of attorneys. During 1958, the Board of Immigration 
Appeals and the Immigration and Naturalization Serv- 
ice eliminated their formal procedures for the ad- 
mission of attorneys to practice before them^ (See 
8 C.F.R. Pt. 292, 23 Fed. Reg. 2670.) This means 
that with the exception of the Interstate Commerce 
Commission, the Patent Office (patent cases), the 
Treasury Department and the Veterans Administra- 
tion, an attorney in good standing may represent 
others before administrative agencies without further 
qualification. 

However, while attorneys should not be subject 
to further qualification requirements, it by no means 
follows that they should be free fronn regulation by the 
agencies. Provisions should be made for simple, 
adequate, and uniform agency rules for discipline 
of attorneys who are guilty of contemptuous conduct 
or who fail to live up to the ethical standards of the 
legal profession. 

A suggested uniform rule on discipline of attor- 
neys, which includes both substantive and procedural 
provisions, is included in the Appendix of this report. 
Also printed is a chart setting forth the provisions of 
present agency rules on disbarment of attorneys. 



UNIFORM RULE STUDIES 

Printed in the Appendix are the texts of the 
illustrative uniform rules recommended in the Report 
of the President's Conference on Administrative 
Procedure on computation of time and appeals from 
intermediate decisions, together with an analysis of 
existing agency rules on each subject. This brings to 
six the nunaber of recomnnendations of the President's 
Conference on uniform illustrative rules which have 
been analyzed to determine the extent of their adoption 
by the agencies. The President's Conference also 
suggested uniform rules on presumptions, stipula- 
tions and admissions of record in proceedings involv- 
ing numerous parties, and form and content of 
decisions. The text of these rules are also set forth. 



PART n- -STATISTICS ON ADMINISTRATIVE 
PROCEEDINGS BEFORE DEPARTMENTS AND 
AGENCIES OF THE FEDERAL GOVERNMENT 

Upon recommendation of the President's Con- 
ference on Administrative Procedure, the Attorney 
General has directed, as one of the functions of this 
Office, the collection and publication of facts and 
statistics concerning administrative procedures of 
the departments and agencies of the Federal govern- 
ment. Initial compliance with this directive last year 
resulted in the publication as a part of our 1957 
Annual Report of nine tables which provided funda- 
mental information relative to formal proceedings 
before hearing examiners appointed pursuant to Sec- 
tion 11 of the Administrative Procedure Act in the 21 
departments and agencies which regularly employ 
such examiners. 

In further performance of this function and for use 
in connection with continuing studies of the adequacy 
and efficiency of administrative procedures, there 
are presented in the twelve tables which follow 
additional and somewhat more comprehensive data 
relative to the sanne kinds of formal proceedings 
before the same 21 departments and agencies covered 
in the 1957 report. 

This report, like the 1957 report, is limited to 
administrative proceedings in which: 

(1) opportunity is afforded for hearing before an 
APA Sec. 11 examiner, and 

(2) such examiner, in normal course, prepares a 
decision or initial or recommended decision. 

Thus proceedings wherein hearings are conducted by 
the agency itself, or by mennbers of its staff other 
than APA exanniners, are not included. Also, these 
tables (with the exception of Table 12) do not include 
proceedings in which, in ordinary course, the exam- 
iner merely conducts the hearing and certifies the 
record to the agency for action, without the examiner's 
having to prepare an initial or recommended deci- 
sion. 



The tables in last year's report were limited 
further as to the stages of proceedings for which 
data were reported. The 1957 tables encompassed 
only so much of the above -described proceedings as 
was in the hands of hearing exanainers, thereby ex- 
cluding whatever stages preceded assignment of a 
case to an examiner as well as agency consideration 
of the matter following the exanniner*s decision. 

Only the second part of the tables presented here- 
with (Part B, Tables 7-11) are subject to this limita- 
tion. Tables 7-11 represent a continuation of last 
year's report relative to process before hearing 
examiners. Part A of these statistics for fiscal 1958 
(Tables 1-6) enlarge upon the information furnished 
last year by including all stages of proceedings 
described in (1) and (2) above, from commencement 
to final agency disposition. More specifically, if a pro- 
ceeding was pending in any stage at any time during 
the fiscal year, information thereon is included in 
Part A of these tables. If it was in the hands of a 
hearing examiner at any time during the year, 
additional information concerning its handling by the 
exanminer, similar to the information in last year's 
tables, is included in Part B. 

As used herein, the ternn ''proceeding" means a 
matter which in normal course is referred to an 
examiner for evidentiary hearing, by oral or written 
presentation, and for recommended or initial decision 
by him, even though it may be a consolidated proceed- 
ing involving more than one petition, application, or 
complaint. Therefore, although a matter may involve 
several claimants, applicants, or respondents and 
several docket numbers, if it looks toward resolution 
by means of a joint hearing and a single decision 
binding upon all parties, it is reported as one * 'pro- 
ceeding." 

The question of when a matter becomes a "pro- 
ceeding", that is, when it is commenced, for purposes 
of this report is important in connection with Part A. 
Under some administrative procedures, a proceeding 
as herein defined is commenced upon the filing of a 
complaint, petition, application, etc., as in the case 
of court actions. These are cases in which opportunity 



for hearing ordinarily is afforded on every petition or 
complaint filed. For purposes of this report these 
cases are counted as proceedings from the date of 
initial filing, and the age of such proceedings is 
computed from that date. 

Other procedures do not necessarily contemplate 
hearing in every case. For example, depending upon 
statutory authority, petitions or applications are often 
subject to summary administrative grant or denial 
without hearing, and are designated as proceedings in 
which there will be a hearing only where issues are 
presented which preclude summary administrative 
determination. These matters are not counted as 
proceedings for the purposes of this report until 
they reach the point where a determination is made 
that they are to be resolved in ordinary course by the 
hearing process. In these cases, the date of that 
determination, whether it be by designation for hear- 
ing, entry upon the hearing docket, or referral to the 
office of hearing examiners, is considered the date 
upon which the proceeding was commenced, and its 
age is computed therefronri. 

The division into types of proceedings which is 
employed in these tables is a division made by the 
reporting agencies, although the characterizations 
have been abbreviated for purposes of the tables. 

The separate listing of data by agency and by type 
of proceeding within each agency may tend to en- 
courage comparisons between agencies and types of 
proceedings. Although it is believed that the informa- 
tion presented affords some opportunity for valid and 
useful comparison, obviously any conaparison should 
be attempted only with full recognition of the many 
significant differences among the various agency 
proceedings. 

PART A 

Table 1 is intended to provide a general view of 
the entire **formal case'' process in terms of each 
agency's volume and backlog, by type of proceeding. 
The first column indicates the number of pending 
cases with which each agency began the fiscal year, 



whatever their stage of completion. The second and 
third columns report the year's input in new cases 
and cases which at some time had been closed but 
which, during the fiscal year, were reopened. The 
fourth column shows the output, and the fifth column 
shows the balance remaining at the end of the fiscal 
year. The grand totals at the end of the table demon- 
strate that there were 16,582 proceedings of all types 
in process before the 21 reporting agencies on July I, 
1957, that 32,783 proceedings were commenced or 
reopened during the fiscal year, that 25,824 were 
closed during that time, and that a balance of 23,541 
proceedings were still pending on June 30, 1958. 

It is to be noted that although the total number of 
pending cases of all types increased from 16,582 at 
the beginning of the fiscal year to 23,541 at the end of 
the year, an increase of 6,959 cases, 5,473 of these 
cases were appeals from benefits rights determina- 
tions in the Social Security Administration due to the 
extension of disability insurance benefits under the 
1956 amendments to Title II of the Social Security Act. 
Of the 23,541 proceedings pending at the end of the 
year, 14,355 were Social Security cases. Eliminating 
the Social Security cases from the grand total figures, 
there were 9,186 proceedings pending at the end of 
the year, which represented an increase of 1,486 
over the nunnber pending at the beginning of the year. 

The major increases in pending cases, other than 
in Social Security cases, were in the Federal Power 
Comnnission (from 426 to 956), Federal Communica- 
tions Commission (from 97 to 169), Agriculture 
Department (from 65 to 100), Federal Trade Com- 
mission (from 262 to 326), and Interstate Commerce 
Commission (3,349 to 4,069). 

The total number of cases closed during the fiscal 
year 1958 by all 21 reporting agencies was 25,824. 

Table 2 demonstrates the age of proceedings 
pending at the end of the fiscal year. The age of each 
case was measured from its commencement, as that 
term is described above. The demonstration is by age 
grouping, since it is felt that this technique is perhaps 
more indicative than average or median figures. 

8 



Data as to age of proceedings are not available for 
Social Security cases or for Indian probate matters 
before Examiners of Inheritance in the Interior 
Department. Of the 7,505 cases pending on June 30, 
1958, for which age data are available, more than one 
half (53%) were less than 6 naonths old, slightly less 
than one quarter (24%) were more than 6 months old 
but not more than one year old, 13% were over 1 year 
old but not over 2 years old, and the remaining 10% 
were almost evenly divided between the 2 to 3 years 
old and over 3 years old columns. 

Table 3 indicates the manner in which the agencies 
disposed of the 25,824 proceedings which they closed 
during the fiscal year. The nunnber of proceedings 
decided on the merits by agency final decision after 
hearing and decision by an examiner was 2,843, or 
11% of the total, and an additional 1,205 (4.6%) were 
decided by the agencies without initial or recom- 
naended decision by an examiner. Of the latter group, 
995 were Interstate Commerce Connmission cases 
and 115 were National Labor Relations Board cases. 
In more than one half of the proceedings closed during 
the year the examiner's decision became the final 
decision of the agency without review by the agency 
(13,198 proceedings, or 51% of the total). Of these, 
7,944 were Social Security cases, 1,611 were Indian 
probate matters, 1,424 were Interstate Commerce 
Commission cases, and 1,405 were cases before 
Coast Guard examiners. A total of 7,953 proceedings 
(31%) were dismissed or withdrawn without decision 
on the merits. This figure does not include cases 
which were disposed of by default, consent, settle- 
ment, or compromise. 

Tables 4, 5 and 6 are concerned with the 2,843 
proceedings mentioned above which were disposed of 
after hearing, examiner decision, and agency final 
decision, and provide information relative to the time 
involved in the disposition of such cases. 

Table 4 illustrates the over -all time that such 
matters were pending, frona commencement to final 
closing. These data are not available for the 328 
Social Security cases which were taken up frona the 
examiners to the Appeals Council, Of all of the other 



types of proceedings, 9% were disposed of within 6 
months after their commencement, 42% required 
from 6 months to a year, 39% were disposed of in 
periods of from 1 to 2 years, 6% took from 2 to 3 
years, and 4% required more than 3 years. 

Table 5 dennonstrates the periods of time these 
proceedings were pending after their commencement, 
before they were assigned to examiners. Five agencies 
assign all proceedings to examiners at the time the 
proceedings are commenced. These agencies are the 
Federal Trade Comnnibsion, Social Security Adminis- 
tration, Bureau of Indian Affairs, Office of Alien 
Property, and the Coast Guard. The Civil Aeronautics 
Board makes such assignments in safety enforcement 
proceedings. Of the 2,842 proceedings closed during 
the year by agency final decision after an examiner's 
decision, 21% were assigned to examiners at the time 
they were commenced, 22% were assigned in 1 month 
or less, 33% in 1 to 3 months, 17% in 3 to 6 months, 
5% in 6 to 12 months, and 2% were pending more than 
1 year before they were assigned. 

Table 6 reports the periods of time during which 
these same proceedings were under advisement by 
the agencies. In conaputing time under advisement, the 
period was measured from the date the matter was 
first ripe for agency final decision, that is, the time 
when the presentations to the agency were complete 
(last exceptions to examiner's decision filed, record 
certified, etc.), to issuance of the agency's final 
decision. Such data are not available for the 328 
Social Security cases appealed to the agency. Of the 
2,514 proceedings as to which such data are available, 
41% were decided within 3 months. The agency's 
decisional process required more than one year in 
9% of the cases. 

PART B 

Tables 7-11 repeat the basic information pre- 
sented in the statistical tables in the 1957 report and 
add corresponding new data for the fiscal year 1958. 
They thereby afford comparison of the two years' 
experience under each itenn, 

10 



Part B concerns only those proceedings reported 
in Part A which at any time during the year were in 
the hands of hearing examiners. Further, it encom- 
passes only that period in each proceeding when it 
was in the hands of the exanniner, from the date of 
assignment of the proceeding to an examiner to the 
date its pendency before him terminates, regardless 
of whether that ternnination ultimately disposes of the 
proceeding insofar as the agency is concerned. 

Table 7 gives the number of proceedings of each 
type for each agency pending before examiners on 
July 1, 1956 and July 1, 1957, input during each of 
the fiscal years 1957 and 1958 in terms of new cases 
assigned to examiners and cases remanded or re- 
assigned to examiners, examiners* output during 
each year, and the balance still pending on June 30, 
1957 and June 30, 1958. 

Although comparison of the total number of pro- 
ceedings pending at the end of fiscal 1957 with the 
number pending at the end of 1958 would seem to 
indicate a substantial increase in backlog, this in- 
crease, as in Table 1, is due for the most part to the 
influx of new cases before the Social Security Ad- 
ministration. Eliminating Social Security cases, the 
totals reflect an increase from 4,556 proceedings 
pending at the beginning of fiscal 1958, to 4,913 
pending at the end of the year, or 7.8% increase. The 
num.ber of proceedings other than Social Security 
proceedings disposed of by examiners during the year 
decreased from 10,518 in 1957 to 9,412 in 1958, a 
drop of 11.7%. 

Table 8 demonstrates how long proceedings in the 
hands of examiners at the end of each year had been 
pending before examiners. In each case (except as 
noted in footnote 4) the period of time was measured 
from date of assignment to the examiner to June 30. 
Except for Social Security proceedings and Interior 
Department Indian probate cases, as to which these 
data are not available, approximately 10.9% of the 
cases before examiners at the end of the year had 
been assigned to examiners for over a year, as 
against the corresponding figure of 10.5% for fiscal 
1957. 

11 



Table 9 shows the manner in which pendency of 
proceedings before hearing examiners was terminated 
during the fiscal years 1957 and 1958. Of the 22,736 
cases disposed of by examiners during 1958, 16,278, 
or 71.6%, were decided on the merits after hearing, 
and 5,218, or 23% (of which 4,962 were Social Security 
cases), were dismissed or withdrawn without decision 
on the merits. 

Table 10 concerns only proceedings disposed of by 
examiners during the fiscal years 1957 and 1958 by 
decision on the merits after hearing, and demon- 
strates as to those proceedings the overall time the 
matter was in the hands of an examiner, from assign- 
ment to an examiner to decision by him. Of the pro- 
ceedings for which such information is available for 
1958, 53% were disposed of within 3 months of the 
date of assignment to the examiner, and less than 1% 
required more than 2 years. By comparison 58% were 
disposed of in 3 months or less in 1957. 

Table 1 1 reports, for the same proceedings, the 
time under advisement by an examiner. For this 
purpose time under advisement was measured from 
the date of final submission of the matter to the 
examiner for his decision (last proposed findings and 
brief filed, etc.) to the date the examiner's decision 
was issued. In 1958, 34% of the proceedings reported 
in Table 11 were decided by examiners in 1 month or 
less, 84% in 3 naonths or less, and 96% in 6 months or 
less. The corresponding figures for 1957 are 40% 
within 1 month, 79% within 3 months, and 97% within 
6 months. 

Table 12 , except as to the column showing the 
average number of decisions per man-year, goes 
beyond the limitations described above relative to 
types of proceedings, and considers generally the 
employment of examiners in all kinds of cases. 
Under the first heading the table shows the total 
number of examiners employed at the beginning of 
each of the fiscal years 1956, 1957, and 1958. Of the 
total employed on July 1, 1958, 160 were in grade 
GS-15, 61 were GS-14, 109 were GS-13, and 10 were 
GS-12. 

12 



Figures in the column showing man-years of 
examiner time available to each agency during the 
fiscal years 1957 and 1958 take into account inter- 
agency loans of examiners as well as separations 
from and entrances upon service. 

For purposes of the column which shows the 
average number of days per man -year spent by 
examiners presiding over hearings and pre -hearing 
or hearing conferences, the agencies were asked to 
report as a day of hearing any day on which the 
examiner presided over a hearing or conference, 
whether for the full day or only part of the day, and 
whether in one proceeding or more than one. The 
total number of days thus reported was divided by the 
agency's man-years figure in the preceding column 
to provide the figures shown in the column showing 
average number of days in hearing. 

Sinmilarly, figures in the column showing average 
number of decisions per man -year were computed by 
dividing, for each agency, the total number of exami- 
ner decisions on the merits after hearing, as shown 
in the second column of Table 9, by the man-years 
figure in the second column of this table. The last 
column shows the number of cases assigned to an 
examiner with the intention that he not issue a deci- 
sion but instead merely conduct the hearing and cer- 
tify the record to the agency for its decision. This 
column does not include cases wherein the examiner 
would have issued a decision but for waiver thereof.

\l Except for safety enforcement proceedings, all of the data reported by the CAB include 
actual figures for cases in process, but estimated figures for cases awaiting action* 
The CAB report explains that the nximber of proceedings awaiting action and the number 
of new cases filed are necessarily only judgment figures because it cannot be deter- 
mined in advance that a particular docket will be subject to hearing or how many dockets 
will be consolidated into a single proceeding. For example, the number of route licensing 
proceedings pending at the beginning of the year (reported as' 216) includes 39 actual pro- 
ceedings in process and an estimated 177 proceedings awaiting action^ 

2/ FCC data for Part A are not broken down as to types of proceeding, but are 
" consolidated so that the totals are available.

3/ 



Except for safety enforcement proceedings, all of the data reported by the CAB include 
actual figures for cases in process, but estimated figures for cases awaiting action. 
The CAB report explains that the number of proceedings awaiting action and the number 
of new cases filed are necessarily only judgement figures because it cannot be deter- 
mined in advance that a particular docket will be subject to hearing or how many dockets 
will be consolidated into a single proceeding. 

FCC data for Part A are not broken down as to types of proceeding, but are consolidated 
so that the totals are available. 

Of the 14,355 Social Security cases pending on June 30, 1958, a total of 5,060 had been 
penciing before examiners more than 60 days after receipt of the claims files o The SSA 
estimates that in these 5,060 cases the average time from assignment to an examiner was 
6 or 7 months. 



4/ 



5/ 



All of these cases were suspended for the last eight months of calendar year 1956 

and the entire calendar year 1958, during required reconsiderations of the Communist 
Party case under successive remands from the Courts. 

The last column of Table 1 reports 23,541 proceedings of all types pending on June 30, 1958 
However, this table includes age data on only 7,505 of these proceedings since such data 
are not available for the 14,355 Social Security cases or the 1,681 Indian probate cases 
pending at the end of the year.

Indian probate cases are commenced when the Examiner of Inheritance receives notice from an 
agency Superintendent that an Indian has died. Data on the period from that time to closing 
of the case by the Examiner are not available. Very few of these cases are appealed to the 
agency. The figures in table 4 refer only to the period from receipt of appeal to final 
agency decision in these few cases which were appealed. 

One SACB case was disposed of by final decision in July 1957, reopened shortly thereafter, and 
again disposed of by final decision in December ;195 7. In computing over-all time involved 
for purposes of this table, this case is reported only once, by the time from commencement 
to the first final decision. 

These cases were suspended for the last 8 months of calendar year 195-6, during 
required reconsideration of the Communist Party case under court remand. 

The fifth column of Table 3 reports a grand total of 2,843 proceedings disposed of during the 
vear bv aeency final decision after an examiner's decision. This table presents data on pnly 
2 514 of these proceedings because such data are not available for the 328 Social Security 
appeals, and because the one SACB matter referred to in Footnote 3 above is counted as one 
proceeding in this table whereas it was counted as two proceedings for purposes of Table ?.

1/ CAB medical review and other safety enforcement proceedings are assigned to examiners at the time 
they are commenced^ Other CAB proceedings are assigned after they are commenced^ However, be- 
cause the data as to the periods of time between commencement and assignment are not available, 
the CAB figures in this table indicate, in lieu thereof, the somewhat longer periods of time 
from commencement to opening of the prehearing conference, or if there was no prehearing con- 
ference, to opening of the hearing. We are advised that the period from assignment to an 
examiner to opening of the conference or hearing is generally brief, 

2/ All of the proceedings of this type are assigned to examiners at the time they are commenced, 

V FCC data for Part A are not broken down as to types of proceeding, but are consolidated so that 
the totals are available. 



4/ With one exception, all cases were assigned to examiners at the time of the issuance of complaint , 

5^/ The total number of cases closed during the fiscal year by agency final decision on the merits 
after an examiner's decision (2,842, as shown in the 1st column) exceeds the total number of 
cases reported in the other coltmns by 608, This is because 55 CAB cases, 52 FTC cases, and 
all Social Security cases (328), Indian ptobate cases (11), Alien Property cases (86), and 
Coast Guard cases (76) were assigned to examiners simultaneous with their commencement and 
therefore are not included in any of the columns indicating periods of time between commence- 
ment and assignment.

Table 7 corresponds to Table I of the 1957 Annual Report of this Office^ The figures in the columns 
marked "1957" are taken from last year's Table I, and the columns marked "1958" report correspond- 
ing new data for the fiscal year 1958 » 

Type of proceeding added to those listed last year^ 

Substituted for "CEA and PACA license revocation or suspension, 7 UcS^C* 69, 499h" in the 1957 reporto 

All CAB safety enforcement proceedings, hearing and non-hearing, were combined in a single set of 
figures in the 1957 report « 

Disregarded in over-all computation because these cases are also reported as new cases pending at the 
beginning of the year^ 

Includes 76 cases "disposed of" by consolidationo 

The figure given under "New cases" includes cases pending at the beginning of the year&gt; It is not 
known how many of these cases were assigned to examiners before July 1, 1956o 

Includes 81 cases "disposed of" by consolidation^ 

Includes 20 default cases and 125 cases disposed of by agreements to discontinue^ 

Includes 15 default cases and 88 agreements to discontinue. 

Includes 17 default cases and 7 agreements to discontinue* 

Includes 6 default cases, and 3 agreements to discontinue « 

In 66 of these cases the examiner's decision was waived. 

in 88 of these cases the examiner's decision was waivedo 

Includes cases in which members of the SACB presidedo (Footnotes continued on next page) 



Footnotes to Table 7 (Cont^) ■ 

16 / It was expected that the number of cases pending before examiners at the end of the 
fiscal year 1957, as shown in last yearns report and repeated in the last column 
of this table under the subheading "1957" would be the same as the number reported 
pending at the beginning of fiscal 1958 (first column, under the subheading "1958"). 
However, there are discrepancies in many instances. The greatest difference in 
reported figures was in Indian probate cases before the Interior Department, There, 
260 "pony" claims which had been dormant for some time were counted as pending cases 
in 1957, but were dropped from the report in 1958, Other differences are due to 
changes on the part of the reporting agencies in their respective decisions as to 
what matters should be counted as "proceedings" within our definition of the term 
(Agriculture Department), or to the technique of counting backward from the time 
the report was rendered to the beginning of the fiscal year (NLRB)» In addition, 
different totals are attributable to division and consolidation of cases (Interior) 
^ or removal from pending docket for return to processing.

\j Table 8 corresponds to Table III in the 1957 Annual Report of this Office » The figures in the 
columns marked "1957" are taken from last year's Table III, and the columns marked "1958" re- 
port corresponding new data for the fiscal year 1958, 

2J TyP^ of proceeding added to those listed last year* 

3/ Substituted for "CEA and PACA license revocation or suspension, 7 U^SoCo 69, 499h" in the 1957 
report, 

4/ Except for medical review cind other safety enforcement proceedings, CAB figures for 1958 reflect 
the time from prehearing conference, or if there was no prehearing conference, the time from 
opening of the hearing, instead of the longer period of time from assignment to an examiner^ 
This is because the dates when proceedings were assigned to examiners are not available except 
in safety enforcement proceedings, where assignment to an examiner is made at the time the pro- 
ceeding is commenced^ We are advised that the period from assignment to an examiner to pre- 
hearing conference or hearing is generally brief „ In the 1957 figures, non-=-hearing safety 
enforcement proceedings are included in the figures for hearing cases » Hearing and non-hearing 
cases are separated in the 1958 figures* 

bj Of the 8,907 Social Security cases pending before examiners on June 30, 1957, 2,558 had been be- 
fore examiners more than 60 days after receipt of the claims files « The SSA estimates that 
these cases had been assigned to examiners an average of 5 or 6 months prior to the "end of 
the yearg At the end of fiscal year 1958, 5,060 of the 14,355 cases assigned to examiners 
had been in examiners' hands more than 60 days after receipt of the claims files. The SSA 
estimates that these cases averaged six or seven months after assignment to an examiner.

hj Includes 260 "pony" claims which are considered dormant and which were dropped from the 
figures for fiscal 1958o 

Ij For the 1957 report the Office of Alien Property reported two types of proceedings, "Claims 
on merits" and "Motions to dismiss^," Data are consolidated in this reporto 

%l Ordinarily NLRB cases are not assigned to specific examiners until within a week before 
hearing. Figures appearing in the column headed "Pending before examiners 3 months or 
less - 1958" include a large number of cases which were awaiting assignment to examiners 
but on June 30, 1958 were not yet assigned, pending completion of examiners* itinerarieSo 

£/ Both of these SACB cases were necessarily suspended for the last 8 months of calendar year 

1956 and for the entire calendar year 1958 during required reconsideration of the Communist 
Party case under successive court remands o 
O 10 / Hearings postponed pending disposition of related criminal cases* 

11 / The last column of Table 7 reports 13,683 proceedings pending before examiners at the end of 
fiscal 1957 and 19,268 at the end of 1958 « Table 8 contains data on only 2,924 proceedings 
pending at the end of 1957 and 3,270 at the end of 1958 « The differences result from the 
unavailability of such data for Social Security and Indian probate cases.

11 Table 9 corresponds to Table II in the 1957 report, and figures under the subheading, "1957," 

are taken from that table. The first column, under "1957," presents the totals of the figures 
reported in all columns of last year's Table II, The second column is the same as the first 
column of Table II except that proceedings identifiable as uncontested proceedings have been 
removed to the column entitled "Default, settlement, compromise or consent," a new column 
which did not appear in Table II. The third column, combining the next 4 columns of 
Table II, reports all dismissals and withdrawals, before and after hearing. The fourth 
column presents a combination of the figures reported last year under "Removal from examiner 
for Agency Action," plus those cases reported in last year's table in the column entitled 
"Other" which by footnote were shown to have been disposed of by waiver of examiner's decisiono 
The 5th column has no counterpart in last year's Table II. The figures appearing therein are 
the cases reported last year by footnote as uncontested cases or as having been disposed of by 
default, settlement, compromise or consent. In some instances cases so disposed of may have 
been reported by the agencies as "other" disposition without any footnote, and therefore are 
reported again in the last column rather than in the fifth column of this table. The last 
column contains the figures from the last column of Table II less those cases removed to the 
fourth and fifth columns as described above because of footnotes to last year's Table II. 
Corresponding new data for fiscal 1958 are reported in all columns under the heading, "1958."

1/ Table 10 corresponds to Table VI of the 1957 report. Figures for 1957 which appeared in the first 
two columns of Table VI under the headings, '*1 mo. or less" and "1 to 3 mos.", are combined in 
this report under the heading, "Decision rendered in 3 mos or less." The last two columns in this 
table report cases in the hands of examiners from 1 to 2 years and over 2 years. Last year the 
agencies were asked to report in a single figure all cases in the hands of examiners more than 12 
months. Therefore 1957 cases in this category are reported in the column entitled "Over 1 year 
but not over 2" even though some of them may have been assigned to examiners more than 2 years. 

2/ Type of proceeding added to those listed last year. 

3/ Substituted for "CEA and PACA license revocation or suspension, 7 U.S.C. 69, 499h" in the 1957 
report. 

4/ Except for safety enforcement proceedings, the period of time reflected in the CAB figures is the 
period from opening of the hearing or, if prehearing conference was held, from such prehearing, to 
disposition by the examiner. With respect to safety enforcement proceedings the periods indicated 
are from date of assignment to an examiner. All CAB safety enforcement proceedings , hearing and 
non-hearing, were combined in a single set of figures in the 1957 report, and therefore are re- 
ported again in that fashion in this table under the column heading "1957." 

_5/ For 1957 the FMB computed over-all examiner time from the date the examiner was directed to hold a 
hearing rather than from date of assignment to an examiner.

6^/ Total figure, 148, includes 12 cases not reported in the other columns of this table. These were 
reopened or remanded cases as to which data relative to time involved are not available. 

Ij Includes 3 reopened or remanded cases for which time data not available. 

8^/ Includes 4 cases for which time data not available. 

9^/ Includes 2 cases for which time data not available. 

10 / Includes 25 cases for which time data not available. 

O 11 / Time intervals involved in the 41 Post Office fraud and unlawful order cases decided on the merits 
after hearing in 1957 were not reported separately, but rather were combined with such data for 37 
default cases.

!_/ For purposes of this table the period of time under advisement by an examiner is measured from final 
submission to the examiner, that is, the date the last brief and proposed findings were filed, to 
issuance of the examiner's decision. Corresponding data appeared in the last six columns of Table VIII 
of the 1957 report. Figures for 1957 which appeared in Table VIII in the columns entitled "15 days or 
less" and "16 to 30 days" are combined in this table under the heading, "Under advisement 1 month or 
less." Similarly, figures which appeared last year in the columns entitled "31 to 60 days" and "61 to 
90 days" are combined in this table under the heading "Over 1 month but not over 3." The last two 
columns in this table report cases under advisement by examiners from 6 months to 1 year, and over 
1 year. Last year the agencies were asked to report in a single figure all cases under advisement for 
more than 6 months. Therefore 1957 cases in this category are reported in the column entitled "Over 6 
months but not over 12" even though some of them may have been under advisement for more than 12 months. 

2j Type of proceeding added to those listed in 1957 report. 

3/ Substituted for "CEA and PACA license revocation or suspension, 7 U.S.C. 69, 499h" in the 1957 report. 

4/ All CAB safety enforcement proceedings, hearing and non-hearing, were combined in a single set of 
figures in the 1957 report. 

_5/ Cases reported as being under advisement 90 days or less. Breakout of this figure into shorter periods 
is not available. 



6^/ 1957 data on time from final submission to examiner to issuance of interim report not available. 
Tj Includes estimates in some cases because exact periods are not known.

PRESIDENT'S CONFERENCE ILLUSTRATIVE RULE 

APPEALS FROM INTERMEDIATE DECISIONS 

(a) Notice of Appeal - -Any party may appeal from 
an initial or recommended decision of a hearing 
officer or from a tentative decision of the agency by 
filing a notice of appeal with the agency within ten 
days after service upon him of such decision. 

(b) Exceptions - -An appeal may be perfected by 
filing exceptions to such intermediate decision within 
twenty days after service of the decision. 



115 



(c) Effect of Exceptions --The effectiveness of the 
internnediate decision shall be stayed if any party 
files a notice of appeal and exceptions within the 
times specified above or if the agency upon notice to 
the parties undertakes to review the decision. Other- 
wise, the intermediate decision shall become the 
final decision of the agency, 

(d) Replies - -Within ten days after service of ex- 
ceptions, any opposing party may file a reply to such 
exceptions. 

(e) Content - -Exceptions shall identify specifically 
the findings, conclusions, or proposed action to which 
objection is made, and shall be supported by concise 
argument and by specific page references to the 
parts of the record and the legal and other authorities 
relied upon. 

(f) Enlargement of Time - -The time periods pre- 
scribed by this rule may be extended by the agency 
for good cause.

Saturdays on which the Board's offices are 

not open for business will be considered 

as holidays. 
Saturday is considered a business day.

District of Columbia.

Any document lodged with the Commission after

filing as of the next succeeding business 

day. 
"Legal holiday" means Federal legal holiday. 
"Legal holiday" means national legal holiday. 
Intermediate Saturdays also included. 
When period is less than ten days, intermediate 

Saturdays , Sundays , and holidays are 

excluded.

Explanatory Comment on Column 7 

Seven of the twenty -three agencies which have 
rules relative to the computation of time have a 
provision for the autonaatic extension of the pre- 
scribed period of time when agency documents are 
served upon parties by mail or when parties live in 
designated geographical areas. The rules of three 
agencies^ provide that whenever a prescribed period 
runs from the service of agency notice or other 
document upon him and service is made by mail, 
three days shall be added to the prescribed period.^ 
The rules of a fourth agency^ provides that the three 
days shall be added only when the time allowed for 
filing a response following the service of a document 
by mail is ten days or less. 

Three agencies, by rule, allow additional days 
for filing documents when the parties reside in 
designated geographical areas. The Federal Mari- 
time Board* allows five additional days for filing 
documents when the parties reside in Alaska, Wash- 
ington, Arizona, California or beyond the continental 
United States. The Interstate Commerce Connmis- 
sion^ allows five additional days to any specified 
period when the general office of a person party to a 
proceeding or office of the practitioner representing 
him is located at or west of El Paso, Texas, Salt 
Lake City, Utah, or Helena, Montana. The rules of the 
Securities and Exchange Comnnission® allow five 
additional days to any prescribed period relative to 
filing of documents with it when the hearing officers 
and parties are residents of Montana, Idaho, Wyoming, 
Colorado, New Mexico, Utah, Arizona, Nevada, Wash- 
ington, Oregon and California. Hearing officers and 
parties who reside beyond the confines of the conti- 
nental United States shall have an additional period of 
twenty days within which to file documents with the 
Commission, 

^Indian Claims Commission (25 CFR § 503.5(d)), National Labor Relations 
Board (29 CFR § 102.83) and Subversive Activities Control Board (28 CFR 
§ 201.3). 

*NLRB rules provide that three days shall not be added if any extension of 
such time may have been granted. 

3 Federal Communications Commission (47 CFR § 1,18 (d)). 

^46 CFR §201.102. 

5 49 CFR § 1.21(c). 

6 17 CFR §201.13.

PRESIDENrS CONFERENCE ILLUSTRATIVE RULE 
COMPUTATION OF TIME 

In computing any period of time prescribed or 
allowed by the (agency) rules, by order of the (agency) 
or by any applicable statute, the day of the act, event, 
or default after which the designated period of time 
begins to run is not to be included. The last day of the 
period so computed is to be included, unless it is a 
Saturday, Sunday or a legal holiday, in which event 
the period runs until the end of the next day which is 
neither a Saturday, Sunday nor a holiday. When the 
period of time prescribed or allowed is less than 7 
days, intermediate Saturdays, Sundays and holidays 
shall be excluded- in the computation. 



119 



ADDITIONAL PRESIDENT'S CONFERENCE'S 
SUGGESTED ILLUSTRATIVE RULES 

PRESUMPTIONS 

Upon proof of the predicate facts specified in the 
subrules hereof without substantial dispute and by 
direct, clear, and convincing evidence, the Agency, 
with or without prior request or notice, may make 
the following presumptions, where consistent with all 
surrounding facts and circumstances: 

(i) Continuity , That a fact of a continuous nature, 
proved to exist at a particular time continues 
to exist as of the date of the presumption, if 
the fact is one which usuciUy exists for at 
least that period of time; 

(ii) Identity . That persons and objects of the same 
name and description are identical; 

(iii) Delivery , Except in a proceeding where the 
liability of the carrier for non-delivery is 
involved, that mail matter, conamunications, 
express or freight, properly addressed, 
marked, billed and delivered respectively to 
the post office, telegraph, cable or radio 
company, or authorized common carrier of 
property with all postage, tolls and charges 
properly prepaid, is or has been delivered to 
the addressee or consignee iii the ordinary 
course of business. 

(iv) Ordinary Course . That a fact exists or does 
not exist, upon proof of the existence or non- 
existence of another fact which in the ordinary 
and usual course of affairs, usually and regu- 
larly CO -exists with the fact presumed; 

(v) Acceptance of Benefit . That a person for whom 
an act is done or to whom a transfer is made 
has, does or will accept same where it is 
clearly in his own self-interest so to do; 

120 



(vi) Interference with Remedy ^ That evidence, with 
respect to a material fact which in bad faith 
is destroyed, eloigned, fabricated, suppressed 
or withheld by a party in control thereof, would 
if produced, corroborate the evidence of the 
adversary party with respect to such fact. 

STIPULATIONS AND ADMISSIONS OF RECORD IN 
PROCEEDINGS INVOLVING NUMEROUS PARTIES 

The existence or non-existence of a material 
fact, as made or agreed in a stipulation or in an 
admission of record^ will be conclusively presumed 
against any party bound thereby, and no other evidence 
with respect thereto will be received upon behalf of 
such party, provided: 

(i) Upon Whom Binding . Such a stipulation or ad- 
mission is binding upon the parties by whom 
it is naade, their privies and upon all other 
parties to the proceeding who do not expressly 
and unequivocally deny the existence or non- 
existence of the material fact so adnnitted or 
stipulated, upon the rnaking thereof, if made 
on the record at a pre -hearing conference, 
oral hearings oral argunn.ent or by a writing 
filed and served upon all parties within five 
days after a copy of such stipulation or ad- 
mission has been served upon them. 

(ii) Withdrawal . Any party bound by a stipulation 
or adnnission of record at any time prior to 
final decision may be permitted to withdraw 
the same in whole or in part by showing to the 
satisfaction of the hearing officer or the 
agency that such stipulation or admission was 
made inadvertly or under a bona fide mistake 
of fact contrary to the true fact and that its 
withdrawal at the time proposed will not 
unjustly prejudice the rights of other parties 
to the proceeding. 



121 



FORM AND CONTENT OF DECISIONS 

Every decision, whether recommended, initial, 
tentative or final, shall contain in the following order: 

(i) A correct caption of the proceeding; 

(ii) Description of the nature and status of the 
decision; 

(iii) Separately identified headnotes of the several 
points decided; 

(iv) Designation of all parties and counsel to the 
proceeding; 

(v) A concise statement of the nature and back- 
ground of the proceeding; 

(vi) A concise statement of the facts but without 
recitation of the evidence; 

(vii) A concise definition of the issues to be 
decided; 

(viii) In separately identified paragraphs (which will 
correspond to the appropriate headnotes) a 
concise discussion of each issue, the prin- 
ciples involved and the determination made 
thereof with reasons and precedents relied 
upon to support the same; 

(ix) A statement of the rule, order, sanction or 
relief or the denial thereof to be made in 
accordance with the decision.

PROPOSED AGENCY RULES ON ADMISSION AND 
DISCIPLINE OF ATTORNEYS" 

I. Admission. 

(a) Practice by Attorneys . Any person who is a 
mennber in good standing of the bar of the Supreme 
Court of the United States or of the highest court of 
any state, territory, or of the District of Columbia, 
and is not under any order of any court suspending, 
enjoining, restraining, disbarring, or otherwise re- 
stricting him in the practice of law, may represent 
others before the agency. 

(b) Signature to Constitute Certificate . When a 
person acting in a representative capacity appears 
in person or signs a paper in practice before this 
agency, his personal appearance or signature shall 
constitute a representation to the agency that under 
the provisions of these regulations and the law he is 
authorized and qualified to represent the particular 
party in whose behalf he acts. Further proof of a 
person's authority to act in a representative capacity 
may be required. 

(c) Service by or upon Attorneys . When any par- 
ticipant in any matter before [name of agency] is 
represented by an attorney -at -law and that fact has 
been made known in writing to the agency, any notice 
or other written comnnunication required or pernaitted 
to be given to or by such participant shall be given to 
or by such attorney. Where any other method of 
service is specifically provided by statute, service 
shall also be made as so provided. If a participant is 
represented by more than one attorney, service by or 
upon any one of such attorneys shall be sufficient. 



*It is recognized that non- lawyers are admitted to practice before a number 
of agencies and that there should be admission and disciplinary procedures 
applicable to them. This suggested uniform rule, does not attempt to deal with 
that problem. However, it is believed that it can be adapted to meet the special 
requirements of each agency. 

125 



II, Disqualification and Suspension. 

(a) The Agency may disqualify, tennporarily or 
permanently, from practice before it any attorney 
who it finds, after notice and opportunity for hearing, 
does not meet the requirements for admission to 
practice set forth in Sec, I above, has been dis- 
qualified, suspended, or disbarred by any other agency 
of the Federal Government, is guilty of unethical or 
unprofessional conduct, or is not qualified to repre- 
sent others before the Agency. A person disqualified 
to practice before the Agency will not be recognized 
in a representative capacity during the period of 
disqualification in any matter before the Agency. 

(b) Contemptuous conduct by an attorney at any 
hearing before the Agency shall be ground for sum- 
mary suspension and exclusion from further par- 
ticipation in the proceeding without notice or oppor- 
tunity for hearing. 

III. Procedures for Discipline of Attorneys . 

(a) Whenever an officer or employee of the agency 
has reason to believe that an attorney representing 
others before the agency has violated any provision of 
these rules, he shall promptly make a written report 
thereof to the General Counsel.* The General Counsel 
shall also receive complaints from attorneys, parties 
or members of the public. The General Counsel either 
on the basis of such information or upon his own 
nnotion where he has cause to believe that an attorney 
has violated any provision of these rules shall 
promptly authorize an investigation of such charges 
or complaints. After investigation, the General Coun- 
sel may decline to take further action on any com- 
plaint or he may initiate formal disciplinary pro- 
ceedings. 

(b) If the General Counsel determines that formal 
disciplinary proceedings should be instituted, he shall 
file with the agency a complaint in which he shall set 



*As herein used, the term ''General Counser* applies to the Chief Legal 
Officer of the agency concerned. 

126 



forth with clarity and specificity the nature of the 
misconduct complained of. The General Counsel shall 
cause a copy of the complaint to be personally served 
on the respondent or have a copy dispatched by 
registered mail to his last known address. 

(c) The respondent shall have ten days after 
receipt of such complaint to file with the agency an 
answer thereto. The answer shall include a specific 
admission or denial of each of the allegations of the 
complaint. The respondent may not challenge the 
connplaint by demurrer or motion but he nniay in- 
corporate in his answer the objection that the agency 
does not have jurisdiction or the objection that the 
acts or omissions alleged in the complaint do not 
constitute misconduct which would warrant the im- 
position of discipline, and, if he desires, the re- 
spondent may request a hearing before the agency 
and such hearing shall be held within sixty days after 
the receipt of the request therefor. 

(d) Respondent shall be deemed to be in default 
if he shall fail to file an answer or to appear at the 
hearing, notice of the time and place of which having 
been given to him in writing by the agency not less 
than 10 days prior thereto. 

(e) The General Counsel shall prosecute the 
charges stated in the complaint at the hearing held 
thereono 

(f) The hearing shall be held before the agency or 
one or more mennbers of the body which comprises 
the agency as the agency shall determine. The hearing 
shall not be open to the public unless requested by 
the respondent. The respondent nnay be represented 
by counsel at the hearing, may offer testimony of 
witnesses and other evidence in his behalf, cross- 
examine witnesses, and shall enjoy such other proce- 
dure as are provided for by agency rule for other 
hearings before the agency. 

A record of evidence introduced at the hearing 
shall be kept and the respondent shall have the 
opportunity to inspect it. 



127 



(g) To. warrant a finding of misconduct in con- 
tested cases, the charges mast be established by a 
preponderance of the evidence, and, in cases where 
the respondent defaults, by prima facie evidence. 

(h) The agency after full consideration of the 
record in the case may, by appropriate order, 
( 1 ) disqualify, temporarily or permanently, the re- 
spondent from practice before it or (2) dismiss the 
charges against him. As soon as practicable after 
the conclusion of the hearing, the agency shall enter 
its order stating its findings of fact, conclusions, 
and disposition of the charges. A copy of the order 
of the agency shall be furnished the respondent. 

(i) If the agency orders the respondent to be dis- 
qualified, temporarily or permanently,-- 

(1) a copy of such order shall be published in 
the Federal Register; 

(2) a copy of such order shall be sent to the 
appropriate authorities in the State, territory, 
or the District of Colunnbia from which the 
respondent derived his license to practice 
law, A transcript of the record shall be made 
available upon payment of cost to such author- 
ities upon their request. The agency shall also 
notify such authorities of the ternnination of 
such disqualification. 



128
